## 1. Introduction

This document is intended to act as a primer for the use of the new Neotoma R package, `neotoma2`, for working with **diatom** data.  Some users may be working with this document as part of a workshop for which there is a Binder instance. The Binder instance will run RStudio in your browser, with all the required packages installed.

If you are using this workflow on its own, or want to use the package directly, [the `neotoma2` package](https://github.com/NeotomaDB/neotoma2) is available on GitHub. This workflow uses the development version (1.1.1.9000 or later), which you can install by running:

```r
install.packages("remotes")
remotes::install_github("NeotomaDB/neotoma2", ref = "dev")
library(neotoma2)
```

This workshop will also require other packages. To maintain the flow of this document we've placed instructions at the end of the document in the section labelled "[Installing packages on your own](#localinstall)".

## 2. Learning Goals

In this tutorial you will learn how to:

* Search for sites using site names and geographic parameters -- [Site Searches](#3-site-searches)
* Filter results by dataset type (diatoms, diatom surface samples, water chemistry) and by whether a record has a chronology -- [Filter Results](#33-filter-records-tabset)
* Start from a list of site IDs in a spreadsheet (or any other file) -- [Sites from a File](#34-sites-from-a-file-tabset)
* Obtain sample information for the selected datasets and harmonize diatom taxonomy -- [Sample Information](#35-pulling-in-sample-data)
* Perform basic analysis including a stratigraphic diagram, change in diatom habit through time, and the link between diatoms and water chemistry (pH) -- [Basic Analysis](#4-simple-analytics)

### 2.1. Getting Help with Neotoma

If you're planning on working with Neotoma, please join us on [Slack](https://join.slack.com/t/neotomadb/shared_invite/zt-cvsv53ep-wjGeCTkq7IhP6eUNA9NxYQ) where we manage a channel specifically for questions about the R package. You may also wish to join our Google Groups mailing list, please [contact us](mailto:neotoma-contact@googlegroups.com) to be added.

### 2.2 Understanding Data Structures in Neotoma

Data in the Neotoma database itself is structured as a set of linked relationships to express the different elements of paleoecological analysis: space and time, raw data, scientific methods and data models. Because paleoecology is such a broad discipline these relationships can be complex, and as such, the database itself is highly structured. If you want to better understand concepts within the database, you can read the [Neotoma Database Manual](https://open.neotomadb.org/manual), or take a look at [the database schema itself](https://open.neotomadb.org/dbschema).

In this workshop we want to highlight two key structural concepts:
  
  1. The way data is structured conceptually within Neotoma (Sites, Collection Units and Datasets).
  2. The way that this structure is adapted within the `neotoma2` R package.

### 2.2.1 Data Structure in the Neotoma Database

![**Figure**. *The structure of sites, collection units and datasets within Neotoma. A site contains one or more collection units. Chronologies are associated with collection units. Data of a common type (pollen, diatoms, vertebrate fauna) are assigned to a dataset.*](images/sitecollunitdataset.png){width=50%}

Data in Neotoma is associated with sites -- specific locations with lat/long coordinates.

Within a **site**, there may be one or more [**collection units**](https://open.neotomadb.org/manual/dataset-collection-related-tables-1.html#CollectionUnits) -- locations at which samples are physically collected within the site. For example, an archaeological **site** may have one or more **collection units**, pits within a broader dig site; a diatom sampling **site** on a lake may have multiple **collection units** -- core sites within the lake basin. Collection units may have higher resolution GPS locations than the site location, but are considered to be part of the broader site.

Data within a **collection unit** is collected at various [**analysis units**](https://open.neotomadb.org/manual/sample-related-tables-1.html#AnalysisUnits). Any data sampled within an analysis unit is grouped by the dataset type (charcoal, diatom, dinoflagellate, etc.) and aggregated into a [**sample**](https://open.neotomadb.org/manual/sample-related-tables-1.html#Samples). The set of samples for a collection unit of a particular dataset type is then assigned to a [**dataset**](https://open.neotomadb.org/manual/dataset-collection-related-tables-1.html#Datasets).

### 2.2.2 Data Structures in `neotoma2`

![**Figure**. *Neotoma R Package diagram. Each box represents a data class within the package. Individual boxes show the class object, its name, its properties, and functions that can be applied to those objects. For example, a `sites` object has a property `sites`, that is a list. The function `plotLeaflet()` can be used on a `sites` object.*](images/neotomaUML_as.svg)

If we look at the [UML diagram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unified_Modeling_Language) for the objects in the `neotoma2` R package we can see that the data structure generally mimics the structure within the database itself.  As we will see in the [Site Searches section](#3-site-searches), we can search for these objects, and begin to manipulate them (in the [Simple Analysis section](#4-simple-analytics)).

It is important to note: *within the `neotoma2` R package, most objects are `sites` objects, they just contain more or less data*.  There are a set of functions that can operate on `sites`.  As we add to `sites` objects, using `get_datasets()` or `get_downloads()`, we are able to use more of these helper functions.

### 2.2.3 Piping in `R` {.tabset}

Piping is a technique that simplifies the process of chaining multiple operations on a data object. It involves using either of these operators: `|>` or `%>%`. `|>` is a base R operator while `%>%` comes from the `tidyverse` ecosystem in R. In `neotoma2` we use `%>%`.

The pipe operator works as a real-life pipe, which carries water from one location to another. In programming, the output of the function on the left-hand side of the pipe is taken as the initial argument for the function on the right-hand side of the pipe. It helps by making code easier to write and read. Additionally, it reduces the number of intermediate objects created during data processing, which can make code more memory-efficient and faster.

Without using pipes you can use the `neotoma2` R package to retrieve a site and then plot it by doing:

```r
# Retrieve the site
plot_site <- neotoma2::get_sites(sitename = "%Swamp%")
# Plot the site
neotoma2::plotLeaflet(object = plot_site)
```

This would create a variable `plot_site` that we will not need any more, but it was necessary so that we could pass it to the `plotLeaflet` function.

With the pipe (`%>%`) we do not need to create the variable, we can just rewrite our code.  Notice that `plotLeaflet()` doesn't need the `object` argument because the response of `get_sites(sitename = "%Swamp%")` gets passed directly into the function.

#### 2.2.3.1. Code


``` r
# get_sites and pipe. The `object` parameter for plotLeaflet will be the
# result of the `get_sites()` function.
get_sites(sitename = "%Swamp%") %>%
  plotLeaflet()
```

## 3. Site Searches

### 3.1. `get_sites()`

There are several ways to find sites in `neotoma2`, but we think of `sites` as being spatial objects primarily. They have names, locations, and are found within the context of geopolitical units, but within the API and the package, the site itself does not have associated information about taxa, dataset types or ages.  It is simply the container into which we add that information.  So, when we search for sites we can search by:

| Parameter | Description |
| --------- | ----------- |
| sitename | A valid site name (case insensitive) using `%` as a wildcard. |
| siteid | A unique numeric site id from the Neotoma Database |
| loc | A bounding box vector, geoJSON or WKT string. |
| altmin | Lower altitude bound for sites. |
| altmax | Upper altitude bound for site locations. |
| database | The constituent database from which the records are pulled. |
| datasettype | The kind of dataset (see `get_table("datasettypes")`) |
| datasetid | Unique numeric dataset identifier in Neotoma |
| doi | A valid dataset DOI in Neotoma |
| gpid | A unique numeric identifier, or text string identifying a geopolitical unit in Neotoma |
| keywords | Unique sample keywords for records in Neotoma. |
| contacts | A name or numeric id for individuals associuated with sites. |
| taxa | Unique numeric identifiers or taxon names associated with sites. |

All sites in Neotoma contain one or more datasets. It's worth noting that the results of these search parameters may be slightly unexpected. For example, searching for sites by sitename, latitude, or altitude will return all of the datasets for the particular site. Searching for terms such as datasettype, datasetid or taxa will return the site, but the only datasets returned will be those matching the dataset-specific search terms. We'll see this later.

#### 3.1.1. Site names: `sitename="%Lake%"` {.tabset}

We may know exactly what site we're looking for ("Arbutus Lake"), or have an approximate guess for the site name (for example, we know it's something like "Arbutus Lake" or "Lake Arbutus", but we're not sure how it was entered specifically), or we may want to search all sites that have a specific term, for example, *Lake*.

We use the general format: `get_sites(sitename="%Lake%")` for searching by name.

PostgreSQL (and the API) uses the percent sign as a wildcard.  So `"%Lake%"` would pick up ["Arbutus Lake"](https://data.neotomadb.org/710) for us, and it would also pick up any site where "lake" is part of a longer word, such as "Lakeside" or "Blake".  Note that the search query is also case insensitive, so you could simply write `"%LAKE%"`.

##### 3.1.1.1. Code


``` r
lake_sites <- neotoma2::get_sites(sitename = "%Lake%")
plotLeaflet(lake_sites)
```

#### 3.1.2. Location: `loc=c()` {.tabset}

The `neotoma` package used a bounding box for locations, structured as a vector of latitude and longitude values: `c(xmin, ymin, xmax, ymax)`.  The `neotoma2` R package supports both this simple bounding box, but also more complex spatial objects, using the [`sf` package](https://r-spatial.github.io/sf/). Using the `sf` package allows us to more easily work with raster and polygon data in R, and to select sites from more complex spatial objects.  The `loc` parameter works with the simple vector, [WKT](https://arthur-e.github.io/Wicket/sandbox-gmaps3.html), [geoJSON](http://geojson.io/#map=2/20.0/0.0) objects and native `sf` objects in R.

Here we look for sites using a location. We're using a rough rectangle around the Philadelphia area: southeastern Pennsylvania, southern New Jersey and the coast out to Barnegat Bay.  To work with this spatial object in R we also transformed the `geojson_str` element to an object for the `sf` package.  There are many other tools to work with spatial objects in R. Regardless of how you get the data into R, `neotoma2` works with almost all objects in the `sf` package.


``` r
geojson_str <- '{"coordinates":
  [[[-80.52, 42.27],
    [-79.76, 42.27],
    [-75.36, 42.00],
    [-74.69, 41.36],
    [-74.77, 40.22],
    [-75.79, 39.72],
    [-80.52, 39.72],
    [-80.52, 42.27]]],
  "type":"Polygon"}'

penn_sf <- geojsonsf::geojson_sf(geojson_str)

# Note here we use the `all_data` flag to capture all the sites.
penn_sites <- neotoma2::get_sites(loc = penn_sf, all_data = TRUE)
```

You can always simply `plot()` the `sites` objects, but you will lose some of the geographic context.  The `plotLeaflet()` function returns a `leaflet()` map, and allows you to further customize it, or add additional spatial data (like our original bounding polygon, `penn_sf`, which works directly with the R `leaflet` package):

##### 3.1.2.1. Code


``` r
neotoma2::plotLeaflet(penn_sites) %>%
  leaflet::addPolygons(
    map = .,
    data = penn_sf,
    color = "green"
  )
```

#### 3.1.3. Site Helpers {.tabset}

If we look at the [data structure diagram](#222-data-structures-in-neotoma2) for the objects in the `neotoma2` R package we can see that there are a set of functions that can operate on `sites`.  As we add to `sites` objects, using `get_datasets()` or `get_downloads()`, we are able to use more of these helper functions.

As it is, we can take advantage of functions like `summary()` to get a more complete sense of the types of data we have in `penn_sites`.  The following code gives the summary table. We do some R magic here to change the way the data is displayed (turning it into a `datatable()` object), but the main piece is the `summary()` call.

##### 3.1.3.1. Code


``` r
# Give information about the sites themselves, site names &cetera.
neotoma2::summary(penn_sites)
# Give the unique identifiers for sites, collection units and datasets found at
# those sites.
neotoma2::getids(penn_sites)
```


In this document we list only the first 10 records (there are more, you can use `length(penn_sites)` to see how many sites you've got). The `dataset_types` column shows what each site holds: mostly diatoms and water chemistry, but also pollen, geochronologic data and other types. We can see that there are no chronologies associated with the `site` objects (`n_chronologies` is 0). This is because, at present, we have not pulled in the `dataset` information we need. In Neotoma, a chronology is associated with a collection unit (and that metadata is pulled by `get_datasets()` or `get_downloads()`). All we know from `get_sites()` are the kinds of datasets we have and the location of the sites that contain the datasets.

### 3.2. Searching for datasets: {.tabset}

We know that within Neotoma, collection units and datasets are contained within sites.  Similarly, a `sites` object contains `collectionunits` which contain `datasets`. From the table above we can see that the sites we've looked at contain a mix of dataset types. We could write something like this: `table(unlist(strsplit(summary(penn_sites)$dataset_types, ",")))` to see the different datasettypes and their counts.

With a `sites` object we can directly call `get_datasets()`, to pull in more metadata about the datasets.  The `get_datasets()` method also supports any of the search terms listed above in the [Site Search](#3-site-searches) section. At any time we can use `datasets()` to get more information about any datasets that a `sites` object may contain.  Compare the output of `datasets(penn_sites)` to the output of a similar call using the following:

#### 3.2.1. Code


``` r
penn_datasets <- neotoma2::get_datasets(penn_sites)

datasets(penn_datasets)

# How many datasets of each type are there?
table(datasets(penn_datasets)$datasettype)
```

No counts at 30 cm due to poor pollen preservation.",null,"Recorded values were derived by applying percentages to a pollen sum.",null,"Sample levels are varve years, not cm.",null,"Microscope slides are in the Academy of Natural Sciences Diatom Herbarium.  The set of taxonomic names used by Noma Ann Roberts, who did the original counts, was updated to taxonomy used in the PIRLA project by Jacquiline Huvane.  This was done during a workshop that also involved Sushil Dixit.  PIRLA = Paleoecological Investigation of Recent Lake Acidification.  At least 1000 valves were counted per sample.\r\n\r\nResults of the Pocono calibration study have not been published, though a manuscript was prepared.  Detailed and extensive documentation of the project are on paper and archived in the ANSP Library archives.","Results of the Pocono calibration study have not been published, though a manuscript was prepared.  Detailed and extensive documentation of the project are on paper and archived in the ANSP Library archives.\r\n\r\nAll water chemistry values are averages for samples collected from 1983 - 1985.  Total phosphorus data were not uploaded.  Values for all lakes were less than the detection level of 20 ug/L.  Aluminum values are Total Aluminum.\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.",null,null,"Microscope slides are located in the Academy of Natural Sciences Diatom Herbarium.  The Herbarium also has much written data collected by John Sherman.\r\n\r\nThere are no publications for this study.  Investigations were performed at the Academy of Natural Sciences of Philadelphia.  Dr. John Sherman was the lead investigator.  Work was done as part of a larger study to assess acidification of Pocono Mountan lakes.  Other cores were studied and a surface sample calibration dataset was developed.  The original diatom taxonomy was uspdated by Dr. Jacquiline Huvane to be consistent with that used in the PIRLA project (Paleoecological Investigation of Recent Lake Acidification).\r\n\r\nEmily Russell analyzed pollen from this core.  Pb-210 dating was done by Dan Engstrom.  Limnological data available at: https://sc.lib.miamioh.edu/handle/2374.MIA/6368 - as of 16 Aug 2019.","Microscope slides are in the Academy of Natural Sciences Diatom Herbarium. At least 1000 valves were counted per sample.\r\n\r\nResults of the Pocono calibration study have not been published, though a manuscript was prepared. Names have not been lumped.\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","Water chemistry samples were collected from the epilimnion using a 2 m PVC tube.  Sampling depth was 2 m unless euphotic zone (Secchi disk depth x 2) was &lt;2m, in which case, water sampling depth was equal to the euphotic zone.  All methods are described in EPA NLA publications.  Basic measurement data were added to Neotoma; flags and tags were not. Names have not been lumped.\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","Taxonomic nomenclature is that origninally used by the analysts.  Names have not been lumped.\r\n“Water chemistry samples were collected from the epilimnion using a 2 m PVC tube.  Sampling depth was 2 m unless euphotic zone (Secchi disk depth x 2) was &lt;2m, in which case, water sampling depth was equal to the euphotic zone.  All methods are described in EPA NLA publications.  Basic measurement data were added to Neotoma; flags and tags were not.  Data and documentation are available from the EPA website https://www.epa.gov/national-aquatic-resource-surveys/data-national-aquatic-resource-surveys (as of April 2019).”\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","All lakes were sampled as part of the US EPA's 2017 National Lake Assessment.\r\n\r\nSurface sediment samples were collected by crews from individual state agencies at the time they were doing other sampling for the EPA National Lake Survey.  Samples from sediment core intervals 0-1 and 1-2 cm were both saved.  Diatom analysis was performed on the combined material from both intervals (0-2 cm).  No core material was saved for future analysis.\r\n\r\nNo data on lake or watershed characteristics were available from EPA at the time data were entered into Neotoma (November 2018).  Data are expected to be available in the future at: https://www.epa.gov/national-aquatic-resource-surveys/nla\r\n-------\r\n\r\nFrom NE Voucher flora website:https://diatoms.org/projects/northeast-lakes-regional-flora\r\n\r\n\"This project is a model for ensuring taxonomic consistency across analysts by 1) use of Battarbee Chambers, 2) randomizing sample assignment to analysts, 3) developing a pre-voucher flora, 4) coordinating analysts through weekly webinars, 5) ensuring 20% repeat counts (10% and self 10% cross QA/QC), 6) documenting taxon names and sources, and 7) thorough project documentation.\r\n\r\n1) Sediments from 106 lakes in the northeast were processed to produce quantitative slides, through the use of Battarbee Chambers.\r\n\r\n2) The samples, along with 6 replicate samples, were randomized for assignment to 3 analysts.\r\n\r\n3) A pre-voucher flora based on over 4000 digital images was developed to represent the taxonomic diversity and morphologic range of species.\r\n\r\n4) Seven weekly webinars were held for analysts to discuss problematic taxa and highlight new taxa added to the flora as they were encountered. Newly encountered taxa were imaged and incorporated into a weekly updated flora. We led a workshop with the analysts and other interested experts at the Diatom Herbarium at the Academy of Natural Sciences in Philadelphia on March 27-30, 2018 to assign species names and resolve difficult species boundaries in this project. \r\n\r\n5) A rigorous QA/QC procedure documents analyst self consistency and cross analyst consistency.\r\n\r\n6, 7) The flora, taxon names, references and R scripts documenting the project and preparing the data for analysis are available at the INSTAAR link. \r\n\r\nFunding\r\nUS Environmental Protection Agency\r\nRegional Voucher Flora and taxon webpages for North Eastern Lakes - Sarah Spaulding\r\n--------------\r\nA set of slides is archived in the Diatom Herbarium at the Academy of Natural Sciences\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","All lakes were sampled as part of the US EPA's 2017 National Lake Assessment.\r\n\r\nSurface sediment samples were collected by crews from individual state agencies at the time they were doing other sampling for the EPA National Lake Survey.  Samples from sediment core intervals 0-1 and 1-2 cm were both saved.  Diatom analysis was performed on the combined material from both intervals (0-2 cm).  No core material was saved for future analysis.\r\n\r\nNo data on lake or watershed characteristics were available from EPA at the time data were entered into Neotoma (November 2018).  Data are expected to be available in the future at: https://www.epa.gov/national-aquatic-resource-surveys/nla\r\n-------\r\n\r\nFrom NE Voucher flora website:https://diatoms.org/projects/northeast-lakes-regional-flora\r\n\r\n\"This project is a model for ensuring taxonomic consistency across analysts by 1) use of Battarbee Chambers, 2) randomizing sample assignment to analysts, 3) developing a pre-voucher flora, 4) coordinating analysts through weekly webinars, 5) ensuring 20% repeat counts (10% and self 10% cross QA/QC), 6) documenting taxon names and sources, and 7) thorough project documentation.\r\n\r\n1) Sediments from 106 lakes in the northeast were processed to produce quantitative slides, through the use of Battarbee Chambers.\r\n\r\n2) The samples, along with 6 replicate samples, were randomized for assignment to 3 analysts.\r\n\r\n3) A pre-voucher flora based on over 4000 digital images was developed to represent the taxonomic diversity and morphologic range of species.\r\n\r\n4) Seven weekly webinars were held for analysts to discuss problematic taxa and highlight new taxa added to the flora as they were encountered. Newly encountered taxa were imaged and incorporated into a weekly updated flora. We led a workshop with the analysts and other interested experts at the Diatom Herbarium at the Academy of Natural Sciences in Philadelphia on March 27-30, 2018 to assign species names and resolve difficult species boundaries in this project. \r\n\r\n5) A rigorous QA/QC procedure documents analyst self consistency and cross analyst consistency.\r\n\r\n6, 7) The flora, taxon names, references and R scripts documenting the project and preparing the data for analysis are available at the INSTAAR link. \r\n\r\nFunding\r\nUS Environmental Protection Agency\r\nRegional Voucher Flora and taxon webpages for North Eastern Lakes - Sarah Spaulding\r\n--------------\r\nA set of slides is archived in the Diatom Herbarium at the Academy of Natural Sciences\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","“Water chemistry samples were collected from the epilimnion using a 2 m PVC tube.  Sampling depth was 2 m unless euphotic zone (Secchi disk depth x 2) was &lt;2m, in which case, water sampling depth was equal to the euphotic zone.  All methods are described in EPA NLA publications.  Basic measurement data were added to Neotoma; flags and tags were not.  Data and documentation are available from the EPA website https://www.epa.gov/national-aquatic-resource-surveys/data-national-aquatic-resource-surveys (as of April 2019).”\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","The Academy of Natural Sciences of Philadelphia Diatom Herbarium has slides from the eastern and midwestern parts of the US.\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","The Academy of Natural Sciences of Philadelphia Diatom Herbarium has slides from the eastern and midwestern parts of the US.\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","All lakes were sampled as part of the US EPA's 2017 National Lake Assessment.\r\n\r\nSurface sediment samples were collected by crews from individual state agencies at the time they were doing other sampling for the EPA National Lake Survey. No core material was saved for future analysis.\r\n\r\nNo data on lake or watershed characteristics were available from EPA at the time data were entered into Neotoma (November 2018). We led a workshop with the analysts and other interested experts at the Diatom Herbarium at the Academy of Natural Sciences in Philadelphia on March 27-30, 2018 to assign species names and resolve difficult species boundaries in this project. \r\n\r\n5) A rigorous QA/QC procedure documents analyst self consistency and cross analyst consistency.\r\n\r\n6, 7) The flora, taxon names, references and R scripts documenting the project and preparing the data for analysis are available at the INSTAAR link. \r\n\r\nFunding\r\nUS Environmental Protection Agency\r\nRegional Voucher Flora and taxon webpages for North Eastern Lakes - Sarah Spaulding\r\n--------------\r\nA set of slides is archived in the Diatom Herbarium at the Academy of Natural Sciences\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","All lakes were sampled as part of the US EPA's 2017 National Lake Assessment.\r\n\r\nSurface sediment samples were collected by crews from individual state agencies at the time they were doing other sampling for the EPA National Lake Survey. Data are expected to be available in the future at: https://www.epa.gov/national-aquatic-resource-surveys/nla\r\n-------\r\n\r\nFrom NE Voucher flora website:https://diatoms.org/projects/northeast-lakes-regional-flora\r\n\r\n\"This project is a model for ensuring taxonomic consistency across analysts by 1) use of Battarbee Chambers, 2) randomizing sample assignment to analysts, 3) developing a pre-voucher flora, 4) coordinating analysts through weekly webinars, 5) ensuring 20% repeat counts (10% and self 10% cross QA/QC), 6) documenting taxon names and sources, and 7) thorough project documentation.\r\n\r\n1) Sediments from 106 lakes in the northeast were processed to produce quantitative slides, through the use of Battarbee Chambers.\r\n\r\n2) The samples, along with 6 replicate samples, were randomized for assignment to 3 analysts.\r\n\r\n3) A pre-voucher flora based on over 4000 digital images was developed to represent the taxonomic diversity and morphologic range of species.\r\n\r\n4) Seven weekly webinars were held for analysts to discuss problematic taxa and highlight new taxa added to the flora as they were encountered. Newly encountered taxa were imaged and incorporated into a weekly updated flora. We led a workshop with the analysts and other interested experts at the Diatom Herbarium at the Academy of Natural Sciences in Philadelphia on March 27-30, 2018 to assign species names and resolve difficult species boundaries in this project. \r\n\r\n5) A rigorous QA/QC procedure documents analyst self consistency and cross analyst consistency.\r\n\r\n6, 7) The flora, taxon names, references and R scripts documenting the project and preparing the data for analysis are available at the INSTAAR link. \r\n\r\nFunding\r\nUS Environmental Protection Agency\r\nRegional Voucher Flora and taxon webpages for North Eastern Lakes - Sarah Spaulding\r\n--------------\r\nA set of slides is archived in the Diatom Herbarium at the Academy of Natural Sciences\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","All lakes were sampled as part of the US EPA's 2017 National Lake Assessment.\r\n\r\nSurface sediment samples were collected by crews from individual state agencies at the time they were doing other sampling for the EPA National Lake Survey.  Samples from sediment core intervals 0-1 and 1-2 cm were both saved.  Diatom analysis was performed on the combined material from both intervals (0-2 cm).  No core material was saved for future analysis.\r\n\r\nNo data on lake or watershed characteristics were available from EPA at the time data were entered into Neotoma (November 2018).  Data are expected to be available in the future at: https://www.epa.gov/national-aquatic-resource-surveys/nla\r\n-------\r\n\r\nFrom NE Voucher flora website:https://diatoms.org/projects/northeast-lakes-regional-flora\r\n\r\n\"This project is a model for ensuring taxonomic consistency across analysts by 1) use of Battarbee Chambers, 2) randomizing sample assignment to analysts, 3) developing a pre-voucher flora, 4) coordinating analysts through weekly webinars, 5) ensuring 20% repeat counts (10% and self 10% cross QA/QC), 6) documenting taxon names and sources, and 7) thorough project documentation.\r\n\r\n1) Sediments from 106 lakes in the northeast were processed to produce quantitative slides, through the use of Battarbee Chambers.\r\n\r\n2) The samples, along with 6 replicate samples, were randomized for assignment to 3 analysts.\r\n\r\n3) A pre-voucher flora based on over 4000 digital images was developed to represent the taxonomic diversity and morphologic range of species.\r\n\r\n4) Seven weekly webinars were held for analysts to discuss problematic taxa and highlight new taxa added to the flora as they were encountered. Newly encountered taxa were imaged and incorporated into a weekly updated flora. We led a workshop with the analysts and other interested experts at the Diatom Herbarium at the Academy of Natural Sciences in Philadelphia on March 27-30, 2018 to assign species names and resolve difficult species boundaries in this project. \r\n\r\n5) A rigorous QA/QC procedure documents analyst self consistency and cross analyst consistency.\r\n\r\n6, 7) The flora, taxon names, references and R scripts documenting the project and preparing the data for analysis are available at the INSTAAR link. \r\n\r\nFunding\r\nUS Environmental Protection Agency\r\nRegional Voucher Flora and taxon webpages for North Eastern Lakes - Sarah Spaulding\r\n--------------\r\nA set of slides is archived in the Diatom Herbarium at the Academy of Natural Sciences\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","“Water chemistry samples were collected from the epilimnion using a 2 m PVC tube.  Sampling depth was 2 m unless euphotic zone (Secchi disk depth x 2) was &lt;2m, in which case, water sampling depth was equal to the euphotic zone.  All methods are described in EPA NLA publications.  Basic measurement data were added to Neotoma; flags and tags were not.  Data and documentation are available from the EPA website https://www.epa.gov/national-aquatic-resource-surveys/data-national-aquatic-resource-surveys (as of April 2019).”\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","The Academy of Natural Sciences of Philadelphia Diatom Herbarium has slides from the eastern and midwestern parts of the US.\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","The Academy of Natural Sciences of Philadelphia Diatom Herbarium has slides from the eastern and midwestern parts of the US.\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","“Water chemistry samples were collected from the epilimnion using a 2 m PVC tube.  Sampling depth was 2 m unless euphotic zone (Secchi disk depth x 2) was &lt;2m, in which case, water sampling depth was equal to the euphotic zone.  All methods are described in EPA NLA publications.  Basic measurement data were added to Neotoma; flags and tags were not.  Data and documentation are available from the EPA website https://www.epa.gov/national-aquatic-resource-surveys/data-national-aquatic-resource-surveys (as of April 2019).”\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","“Water chemistry samples were collected from the epilimnion using a 2 m PVC tube. The set of taxonomic names used by Noma Ann Roberts, who did the original counts, was updated to taxonomy used in the PIRLA project by Jacquiline Huvane.  This was done during a workshop that also involved Sushil Dixit.  PIRLA = Paleoecological Investigation of Recent Lake Acidification.  At least 1000 valves were counted per sample.\r\n\r\nResults of the Pocono calibration study have not been published, though a manuscript was prepared.  Detailed and extensive documentation of the project are on paper and archived in the ANSP Library archives.","Results of the Pocono calibration study have not been published, though a manuscript was prepared. Total phosphorus data were not uploaded.  Values for all lakes were less than the detection level of 20 ug/L. At least 1000 valves were counted per sample.\r\n\r\nResults of the Pocono calibration study have not been published, though a manuscript was prepared.  Detailed and extensive documentation of the project are on paper and archived in the ANSP Library archives.","Results of the Pocono calibration study have not been published, though a manuscript was prepared.  Detailed and extensive documentation of the project are on paper and archived in the ANSP Library archives.\r\n\r\nAll water chemistry values are averages for samples collected from 1983 - 1985.  Total phosphorus data were not uploaded.  Values for all lakes were less than the detection level of 20 ug/L. Aluminum values are Total Aluminum.\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","From Dixit et al 1999:\r\n\"In addition to various limnological measurements made in the field, water samples were collected for detailed chemical analysis (Baker et al. 1997). These water samples were taken from the 1.5-m water depth, or from the 0.5-m depth if the lake was &lt;2 m deep. The details of field sampling protocols and methods used for the chemical analysis in EMAP-SW are presented in Baker et al. (1997). The EMAP-SW resource group provided all the necessary physical, chemical, and land use data for the study lakes. These data can be found on EMAP’s web site (http://www.epa.gov/emap/ html/dataI/surfwatr/data/nelakes).\"\r\n\r\nEach lake was sampled once at the same time the core was taken.\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","Diatom slides are in the Diatom Herbarium at the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel Univeristy. The NAMPD used only a subset of taxa.","The lake sediment diatom sampling method is outlined in the 2012 National Lakes Assessment Field Operations Manual (EPA, 2012).\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.","The lake sediment diatom sampling method is outlined in the 2012 National Lakes Assessment Field Operations Manual (EPA, 2012).\nThis dataset was originally uploaded to the Academy of Natural Sciences of Drexel University constituent database, which was merged into the Diatom Paleolimnology Data Cooperative (DPDC) constituent database in July 2026.",null,null,null,"Digitized from publication figure","Data digitized from publication"]],"container":"<table class=\"display\">\n  <thead>\n    <tr>\n      <th> <\/th>\n      <th>datasetid<\/th>\n      <th>database<\/th>\n      <th>datasettype<\/th>\n      <th>age_range_old<\/th>\n      <th>age_range_young<\/th>\n      <th>age_units<\/th>\n      <th>recdatecreated<\/th>\n      <th>notes<\/th>\n    <\/tr>\n  <\/thead>\n<\/table>","options":{"scrollX":"100%","dom":"t","columnDefs":[{"className":"dt-right","targets":[1,4,5]},{"orderable":false,"targets":0},{"name":" ","targets":0},{"name":"datasetid","targets":1},{"name":"database","targets":2},{"name":"datasettype","targets":3},{"name":"age_range_old","targets":4},{"name":"age_range_young","targets":5},{"name":"age_units","targets":6},{"name":"recdatecreated","targets":7},{"name":"notes","targets":8}],"order":[],"autoWidth":false,"orderClasses":false}},"evals":[],"jsHooks":[]}</script>

You can see that this provides information only about the specific dataset, not the site! For a more complete record we can join site information from `summary()` to dataset information using `datasets()` using the `getids()` function which links sites, and all the collection units and datasets they contain.

### 3.3. Filter Records {.tabset}

If we choose to pull in information about only certain dataset types, or if there is additional filtering we want to do before we download the data, we can use the `filter()` function.  Neotoma holds several diatom-related dataset types. Run `neotoma2::get_table("datasettypes")` to see the ones available to you. Here we keep `diatom` (sediment cores), `diatom surface sample` (modern samples used to calibrate relationships with the environment) and `water chemistry`.  If we then want only the sediment cores that have a known chronology, we can filter by `datasettype` and by the presence of an `age_range_young`, which would indicate that there is a chronology that defines bounds for ages within the record.

#### 3.3.1. Code


``` r
diatom_types <- c("diatom", "diatom surface sample", "water chemistry")

penn_diatoms <- penn_datasets %>%
  neotoma2::filter(datasettype %in% diatom_types)

penn_cores <- penn_datasets %>%
  neotoma2::filter(datasettype == "diatom", !is.na(age_range_young))

neotoma2::summary(penn_cores)

# We've removed records, so the new objects should be shorter than the original.
length(penn_diatoms) < length(penn_datasets)
length(penn_cores) < length(penn_diatoms)
```

#### 3.3.2. Result


```{=html}
<div class="datatables html-widget html-fill-item" id="htmlwidget-c053baeee5eb8e01ff10" style="width:100%;height:auto;"></div>
<script type="application/json" data-for="htmlwidget-c053baeee5eb8e01ff10">{"x":{"filter":"none","vertical":false,"data":[["1","2","3"],[13222,13319,13321],["Turtle Pond","Crow Hill","Georgia Farm"],["TURTLE10","CROWHI05","GEORGI05"],[0,0,0],[1,1,1],["diatom","diatom","diatom"]],"container":"<table class=\"display\">\n  <thead>\n    <tr>\n      <th> <\/th>\n      <th>siteid<\/th>\n      <th>sitename<\/th>\n      <th>collunit_name<\/th>\n      <th>n_chronologies<\/th>\n      <th>n_datasets<\/th>\n      <th>dataset_types<\/th>\n    <\/tr>\n  <\/thead>\n<\/table>","options":{"scrollX":"100%","dom":"t","columnDefs":[{"className":"dt-right","targets":[1,4,5]},{"orderable":false,"targets":0},{"name":" ","targets":0},{"name":"siteid","targets":1},{"name":"sitename","targets":2},{"name":"collunit_name","targets":3},{"name":"n_chronologies","targets":4},{"name":"n_datasets","targets":5},{"name":"dataset_types","targets":6}],"order":[],"autoWidth":false,"orderClasses":false}},"evals":[],"jsHooks":[]}</script>
```

We can see now that the data table looks different (comparing it to the [table above](#312-result)), and there are fewer total sites. Again, there is no explicit chronology for these records, we need to pull down the complete download for these records, but we begin to get a sense of what kind of data we have.

#### 3.3.3. Which sites have both diatoms and water chemistry? {-}

Many diatom questions, such as reconstructing lake pH or nutrients, need diatom counts *and* measured water chemistry from the same place. We can find those sites by linking each dataset to its site with `getids()`, and then checking which dataset types each site holds. Because we join on `siteid` only, there is no need to match on names or coordinates. We put the three possible outcomes in a fixed order, so the legend colors always mean the same thing.


``` r
# One row per site, flagging which kinds of data it has.
coverage <- getids(penn_diatoms) %>%
  mutate(across(c(siteid, datasetid), as.numeric)) %>%
  inner_join(
    datasets(penn_diatoms) %>%
      as.data.frame() %>%
      mutate(datasetid = as.numeric(datasetid)) %>%
      select(datasetid, datasettype),
    by = "datasetid"
  ) %>%
  group_by(siteid) %>%
  summarise(
    has_diatoms = any(datasettype %in% c("diatom", "diatom surface sample")),
    has_chem = any(datasettype == "water chemistry"),
    .groups = "drop"
  ) %>%
  mutate(coverage = factor(
    case_when(
      has_diatoms & has_chem ~ "Diatoms + water chemistry",
      has_diatoms ~ "Diatoms only",
      TRUE ~ "Water chemistry only"
    ),
    levels = c(
      "Diatoms + water chemistry", "Diatoms only", "Water chemistry only"
    )
  ))

# Add the site names and coordinates, then map.
coverage_sites <- as.data.frame(penn_diatoms) %>%
  mutate(siteid = as.numeric(siteid)) %>%
  distinct(siteid, sitename, lat, long) %>%
  inner_join(coverage, by = "siteid")

pal <- leaflet::colorFactor(
  c("#1b7837", "#e08214", "#2166ac"),
  levels = levels(coverage_sites$coverage)
)

leaflet::leaflet(coverage_sites) %>%
  leaflet::addTiles() %>%
  leaflet::addCircleMarkers(
    lng = ~long, lat = ~lat, color = ~ pal(coverage),
    radius = 6, fillOpacity = 0.8,
    popup = ~ paste0(
      "<b>", sitename, "</b><br>", coverage,
      "<br><a href='http://apps.neotomadb.org/explorer/?siteids=", siteid,
      "' target='_blank'>Explorer link</a>"
    )
  ) %>%
  leaflet::addLegend(
    "bottomright",
    pal = pal, values = ~coverage, title = "Data available"
  )
```

Click a point to see the site name and a link to the [Neotoma Explorer](http://apps.neotomadb.org/explorer/). The table of counts is `table(coverage$coverage)`.

### 3.4. Sites from a File {.tabset}

Often you will not start from a place or a name, but from a **list of sites you already have**: a training set that your lab assembled, the sites in a published paper, or an "aggregate dataset" kept in a spreadsheet. Here we have such a list in `data/aggregate_sites_NorthAmerica.xlsx`: 2,233 North American Neotoma site IDs, in a single column called `Neotoma Site ID`.

Because a list like this can arrive as an Excel sheet, a `csv`, a text file or an `RDS` file, we use a small helper, `read_site_ids()`, that works the same way for all of them. It looks at the file extension, finds the column that holds the site IDs (or you can name it with `col =`), and returns a clean vector of unique integer IDs with the empty cells removed. If it cannot tell which column to use, it stops and lists the column names rather than guessing.


``` r
# A .txt file is either one ID per line, or a delimited table with a header.
read_text_ids <- function(path) {
  lines <- readLines(path, warn = FALSE)
  lines <- trimws(lines[nzchar(trimws(lines))])
  if (all(grepl("^[0-9]+$", lines))) {
    return(as.integer(lines))
  }
  seps <- c(",", "\t", ";", "|")
  counts <- vapply(seps, function(s) {
    lengths(regmatches(lines[1], gregexpr(s, lines[1], fixed = TRUE)))
  }, integer(1))
  readr::read_delim(path, delim = seps[which.max(counts)], show_col_types = FALSE)
}

read_site_ids <- function(path, col = NULL, sheet = 1) {
  if (!file.exists(path)) stop("File not found: ", path, call. = FALSE)
  ext <- tolower(tools::file_ext(path))

  raw <- switch(ext,
    xlsx = ,
    xls = readxl::read_excel(path, sheet = sheet),
    csv = readr::read_csv(path, show_col_types = FALSE),
    tsv = readr::read_tsv(path, show_col_types = FALSE),
    txt = read_text_ids(path),
    rds = readRDS(path),
    stop("Unsupported file type '.", ext, "'. ",
         "Use xlsx, xls, csv, tsv, txt or rds.", call. = FALSE)
  )

  if (!is.data.frame(raw)) {
    ids <- raw # an RDS file that holds a plain vector
  } else {
    if (is.null(col)) {
      # Compare column names ignoring case, spaces and punctuation.
      clean_names <- gsub("[^a-z0-9]", "", tolower(names(raw)))
      hit <- which(clean_names %in% c("siteid", "neotomasiteid"))
      col <- if (ncol(raw) == 1) {
        1
      } else if (length(hit) == 1) {
        hit
      } else {
        stop("Could not tell which column holds site IDs. Columns found: ",
             paste(names(raw), collapse = ", "),
             ". Use col = \"<column name>\".", call. = FALSE)
      }
    }
    ids <- raw[[col]]
  }

  ids <- unique(stats::na.omit(suppressWarnings(as.integer(ids))))
  message(length(ids), " unique site IDs read from ", basename(path))
  ids
}
```

#### 3.4.1. Code

Once we have the IDs, we hand them straight to `get_sites()`. A long vector is no problem: `neotoma2` splits it into several requests for you.


``` r
aggregate_ids <- read_site_ids("data/aggregate_sites_NorthAmerica.xlsx")
aggregate_sites <- neotoma2::get_sites(aggregate_ids)

plotLeaflet(aggregate_sites)
```

Link<\/a>","<b>Medicine Lake<\/b><br><b>Description:<\/b> Northern Great Plains.  Lake in glacial outwash channel. Physiography: Prairie Coteau.The predominant vegetation in the region was tallgrass prairie prior to Euroamerican settlement. The region is poorly drained and contains multiple wetlands and shallow lakes, many of them closed-basin.<br><a href=http://apps.neotomadb.org/explorer/?siteids=1676>Explorer Link<\/a>","<b>Moon Lake<\/b><br><b>Description:<\/b> Kettle lake. Physiography: rolling end moraine. Native vegetaton: transition from tallgrass to mixed grass prairie. Heavily agricultural today.<br><a href=http://apps.neotomadb.org/explorer/?siteids=1711>Explorer Link<\/a>","<b>Wakaw<\/b><br><b>Description:<\/b> NA<br><a href=http://apps.neotomadb.org/explorer/?siteids=1997>Explorer Link<\/a>","<b>Alkaline Lake<\/b><br><b>Description:<\/b> Large pair of lakes separated by a causeway and surrounded by farmlands. The shoreline is sandy with a narrow beach and the lake is surrounded by wetlands. The lake basin is situated on a morraine and is at a topographic low point. This lake experiences substantial wind energy and the beaches of the southern lake are gravelly. Terrestrial Plant Community: grass and farmland with cottonwoods on high ground away from lake.<br><a href=http://apps.neotomadb.org/explorer/?siteids=8556>Explorer Link<\/a>","<b>Bitter Lake<\/b><br><b>Description:<\/b> Glacial kettle lake. From 1995 to 2011, the lake rose ~8 m and expanded from ~1480 ha to >7700 ha in area. Formerly saline, the lake is now a productive fishery. <br><a href=http://apps.neotomadb.org/explorer/?siteids=9651>Explorer Link<\/a>","<b>Eckelson Lake<\/b><br><b>Description:<\/b> Long linear lake occupies a former glacial meltwater channel. ```

We asked for 2233 IDs and got back 2224 sites. A few IDs may not return a site, for example if a site has since been removed from Neotoma.

#### 3.4.3. Narrowing the list {-}

That is a lot of sites. Calls such as `get_datasets()` and `filter()` get slow as a `sites` object grows (they take minutes for a couple of thousand sites), so it pays to **narrow the list first** and only then ask for more detail. `as.data.frame()` on a `sites` object is fast and gives us the coordinates, so we can use `sf` to keep only the aggregate sites inside the Philadelphia rectangle we defined in [section 3.1.2](#312-location-loc-tabset). We then ask Neotoma for just those sites, get their datasets, and keep the diatom and water chemistry datasets (`diatom_types`, from [section 3.3](#33-filter-records-tabset)).

Note that we write `dplyr::filter()` for the table and `neotoma2::filter()` for the `sites` object, so that R never has to guess which one we mean.


``` r
aggregate_points <- aggregate_sites %>%
  as.data.frame() %>%
  dplyr::filter(!is.na(lat), !is.na(long)) %>%
  sf::st_as_sf(coords = c("long", "lat"), crs = 4326, remove = FALSE)

# Keep the sites that fall inside the Philadelphia polygon.
slice_ids <- aggregate_points$siteid[
  lengths(sf::st_within(aggregate_points, penn_sf)) > 0
]

aggregate_slice <- neotoma2::get_sites(slice_ids)

aggregate_datasets <- neotoma2::get_datasets(aggregate_slice) %>%
  neotoma2::filter(datasettype %in% diatom_types)
```

This leaves us with 88 sites from the list. These are mostly sites with a diatom surface sample and a matching water chemistry measurement, which is the basic ingredient of a diatom-based calibration (or *training*) set.


``` r
plotLeaflet(aggregate_slice) %>%
  leaflet::addPolygons(map = ., data = penn_sf, color = "green")
```


### 3.5. Pulling in `sample()` data

Because sample data adds a lot of overhead (the object that includes the datasets with samples is many times larger than the `dataset` alone), we try to call `get_downloads()` after we've done our preliminary filtering. After `get_datasets()` you have enough information to filter based on location, time bounds and dataset type. When we move to `get_downloads()` we can do more fine-tuned filtering at the analysis unit or taxon level.

The following call can take some time, but we've frozen the object as an RDS data file. You can run this command on your own, and let it run for a bit, or you can just load the object in. (`neotoma2` downloads datasets in batches of 25, and remembers what it has already fetched during your session.)


``` r
## This line is commented out because we've already run it for you.
# nolint start
## aggregate_dl <- aggregate_datasets %>% get_downloads(all_data = TRUE)
## saveRDS(aggregate_dl, "data/aggregate_slice_download.RDS")
# nolint end
aggregate_dl <- readRDS("data/aggregate_slice_download.RDS")
```

Once we've downloaded, we now have information for each site about all the associated collection units, the datasets, and, for each dataset, all the samples associated with the datasets.  To extract all the samples we can call:


``` r
all_samp <- samples(aggregate_dl)
```

When we've done this, we get a `data.frame` that is 5422 rows long and 39 columns wide.  The reason the table is so wide is that we are returning data in a **long** format.  Each row contains all the information you should need to properly interpret it:


```
##  [1] "age"             "agetype"         "ageolder"        "ageyounger"     
##  [5] "chronologyid"    "chronologyname"  "units"           "value"          
##  [9] "context"         "element"         "taxonid"         "symmetry"       
## [13] "taxongroup"      "elementtype"     "variablename"    "ecologicalgroup"
## [17] "analysisunitid"  "sampleanalyst"   "sampleid"        "depth"          
## [21] "thickness"       "samplename"      "datasetid"       "database"       
## [25] "datasettype"     "age_range_old"   "age_range_young" "age_units"      
## [29] "recdatecreated"  "datasetnotes"    "siteid"          "sitename"       
## [33] "lat"             "long"            "area"            "sitenotes"      
## [37] "description"     "elev"            "collunitid"
```

For some dataset types, or analyses some of these columns may not be needed, however, for other dataset types they may be critically important.  To allow the `neotoma2` package to be as useful as possible for the community we've included as many as we can.

Our download holds three dataset types, and they use the same table in different ways. In the diatom datasets each row is the count of valves of a taxon, while in the water chemistry datasets each row is a measurement:


``` r
all_samp %>%
  dplyr::count(datasettype, ecologicalgroup, units, name = "rows") %>%
  dplyr::arrange(datasettype, dplyr::desc(rows)) %>%
  head(12) %>%
  DT::datatable(rownames = FALSE, options = list(scrollX = "100%", dom = "t"))
```

```{=html}
<div class="datatables html-widget html-fill-item" id="htmlwidget-284cb24f78ea5c0a4c67" style="width:100%;height:auto;"></div>
<script type="application/json" data-for="htmlwidget-284cb24f78ea5c0a4c67">{"x":{"filter":"none","vertical":false,"data":[["diatom","diatom surface sample","water chemistry","water chemistry","water chemistry","water chemistry","water chemistry","water chemistry","water chemistry","water chemistry","water chemistry","water chemistry"],["DIAT","DIAT","WCHM","WCHM","WCHM","WCHM","ELEM","WCHM","WCHM","WCHM","WCHM","IORG"],["NISP","NISP","μg/L","mg/L",null,"μS/cm","mg/L","m","units","NTU","μmho/cm","μg/L"],[237,4575,184,155,70,45,42,20,20,14,13,11]],"container":"<table class=\"display\">\n  <thead>\n    <tr>\n      <th>datasettype<\/th>\n      <th>ecologicalgroup<\/th>\n      <th>units<\/th>\n      <th>rows<\/th>\n    <\/tr>\n  <\/thead>\n<\/table>","options":{"scrollX":"100%","dom":"t","columnDefs":[{"className":"dt-right","targets":3},{"name":"datasettype","targets":0},{"name":"ecologicalgroup","targets":1},{"name":"units","targets":2},{"name":"rows","targets":3}],"order":[],"autoWidth":false,"orderClasses":false}},"evals":[],"jsHooks":[]}</script>
```

#### 3.5.1. Extracting Taxa {.tabset}

If you want to know what taxa we have in the record you can use the helper function `taxa()` on the sites object. The `taxa()` function gives us, not only the unique taxa, but two additional columns, `sites` and `samples` that tell us how many sites the taxa appear in, and how many samples the taxa appear in, to help us better understand how common individual taxa are.

Because we downloaded water chemistry as well as diatoms, `taxa()` also lists the chemical variables (pH, total phosphorus, and so on). In Neotoma, all of these are *variables*. The `ecologicalgroup` column tells them apart: `DIAT` for diatoms, and `WCHM` (or `ELEM`) for water chemistry.

##### 3.5.1.1. Code


``` r
neotomatx <- neotoma2::taxa(aggregate_dl)
```

##### 3.5.1.2. Results

#### 3.5.2. Understanding Taxonomies in Neotoma {-}

Taxonomies in Neotoma are not as straightforward as we might expect. Diatom identification can be difficult: it depends on the orientation of the valve, the condition of the frustule, the optical quality of the microscope, the analyst's training and the taxonomic reference they used. Diatom names have also changed a great deal over time (many *Achnanthes* are now *Achnanthidium* or *Psammothidium*, for example), and different labs have identified the same valves at different taxonomic levels, from species to genus, or to a varietal level. Many records also carry project-specific codes, such as the `PIRLA` numbers you will see in names like *Eunotia* sp. 31 PIRLA. You can read more about concepts of taxonomy within Neotoma in the Neotoma Manual's [section on Taxonomic concepts](https://open.neotomadb.org/manual/database-design-concepts.html#taxonomy-and-synonymy).

We use the unique identifiers (*e.g.*, `taxonid`, `siteid`, `analysisunitid`) throughout the package, since they help us to link between records. The `taxonid` values returned by the `taxa()` call can be linked to the `taxonid` column in the `samples()` table.  This allows us to build taxon harmonization tables if we choose to. You may also note that the `taxonname` is in the field `variablename`.  Individual sample counts are reported in Neotoma as [`variables`](https://open.neotomadb.org/manual/taxonomy-related-tables-1.html#Variables). A "variable" may be either a species, something like laboratory measurements, or a non-organic proxy, like water chemistry measurements, and includes the units of measurement and the value.

#### 3.5.3. Simple Harmonization {.tabset}

Lets say we want all samples from which *Navicula* taxa have been reported to be grouped together into one pseudo-taxon called *Navicula-undiff*. **NOTE**, this is not an ecologically useful grouping, but used for illustration.

There are several ways of grouping taxa, either directly by exporting the file and editing each individual cell, or by creating an external "harmonization" table (which we did in the prior `neotoma` package).  First, lets look for how many different ways *Navicula* appears in these records. We can use the function `str_detect()` from the `stringr` package to look for patterns, and then return either `TRUE` or `FALSE` when the string is detected:



We can harmonize taxon by taxon a number of different ways. One way would be to get every instance of a *Navicula* taxon and just change them directly. Here we are taking the column `variablename` from the `all_samp` object (this is where the count data is). The square brackets are telling us which rows we're changing, here only rows where we detect `"Navicula"` in the variable name. For each of those rows, in that column, we assign the value `"Navicula undiff."`:



There were originally 96 different taxa identified as being within the genus *Navicula* (including *Navicula*, *Navicula cryptocephala*, and *Navicula cf. heimansii*). The above code reduces them all to a single taxonomic group *Navicula undiff*.

Note that this changes *Navicula* _only_ in the `all_samp` object, not in any of the downloaded objects. If we were to call `samples()` again, the taxonomy would return to its original form.

If we want to have an artifact of our choices, we can use an external table. For example, a table of pairs (what we want changed, and the name we want it replaced with) can be generated, and it can include regular expressions (if we choose):

| original | replacement |
| -------- | ----------- |
| Navicula.* | Navicula-undiff |
| Nitzschia.* | Nitzschia-undiff |
| Eunotia.* | Eunotia-undiff |
| Fragilaria.* | Fragilaria-undiff |
| ... | ... |

We can get the list of original names directly from the `taxa()` call, applied to a `sites` object that contains samples, and then export it using `write.csv()`.


``` r
taxaplots <- taxa(aggregate_dl)
# Save the taxon list to file so we can edit it subsequently.
readr::write_csv(taxaplots, "data/mytaxontable.csv")
```

#### Looking at the Taxonomic Structure {.tabset}

The `taxa` function returns all our taxonomic information, and it provides some additional information, the columns `samples` and `sites` which record the number of samples across all datasets that contain the taxon, and the number of sites with the taxon. The plot
below shows the relationship between samples and sites for the diatoms, which we would expect to be somewhat skewed, as it is.

##### 3.5.3.1. Code


``` r
taxaplots <- taxa(aggregate_dl) %>%
  dplyr::filter(ecologicalgroup == "DIAT")

ggplot(data = taxaplots, aes(x = sites, y = samples)) +
  geom_point() +
  stat_smooth(
    method = "glm",
    method.args = list(family = "poisson")
  ) +
  xlab("Number of Sites") +
  ylab("Number of Samples") +
  theme_bw()
```

##### 3.5.3.2. Result

![**Figure**. *A plot of the number of sites a taxon appears in, against the number of samples a taxon appears in.*](simple_workflow_files/figure-html/PlotTaxonCounts-1.png)

#### 3.5.4. Editing the Taxonomy Table {-}

The plot (above) is mostly for illustration, but we can see, as a sanity check, that the relationship is as we'd expect. There are a large number of taxa that are rarely present, and then several that are quite common.

Exporting the taxon table to a `csv` file allows us to edit the table, filtering and selecting taxa based on contextual information to help you out. In pollen records the `ecologicalgroup` column is very useful for this, but for diatoms every taxon is just `DIAT`, so the information we need has to come from the **name**. Diatom names carry varieties (`var.`), subspecies (`subsp.`), uncertain identifications (`cf.`), and project codes (`PIRLA`), so we will build a translation table with three simple rules:

1. Remove `cf.` and `aff.` (so *Cymbella* cf. *gaeumannii* becomes *Cymbella gaeumannii*).
2. Drop varieties and subspecies (this lumps varieties together, so adjust it if your question needs them).
3. Keep the genus and species when the second word looks like a species name. Otherwise (for *sp.*, *spp.*, or numbered codes) keep only the genus.

We also add a `habit` column (planktonic, tychoplanktonic or benthic) from a short lookup of the most common genera in our data. **The rules and the lookup are deliberately simple, and they are not perfect.** They are here to show you *how* to build and use a translation table, not to tell you the right diatom taxonomy. Diatom specialists will have better rules, so change the lists to match your own references. Genera that are not in the lookup are left empty.


``` r
# 1. Every diatom taxon in the downloads, one row per taxon. We include the
#    sediment cores that we use in section 4, so one table covers both.
core_dl <- readRDS("data/diatom_core_download.RDS")

diatom_taxa <- bind_rows(
  as.data.frame(taxa(aggregate_dl)),
  as.data.frame(taxa(core_dl))
) %>%
  dplyr::filter(ecologicalgroup == "DIAT") %>%
  dplyr::distinct(taxonid, variablename, ecologicalgroup)

# 2. A short, editable lookup of growth habit for the most common genera.
#    Simplified for teaching: use your own references.
planktonic <- c("Aulacoseira", "Asterionella", "Cyclotella", "Discostella")
tychoplanktonic <- c(
  "Fragilaria", "Staurosira", "Pseudostaurosira", "Tabellaria", "Synedra",
  "Melosira"
)
benthic <- c(
  "Navicula", "Nitzschia", "Eunotia", "Achnanthidium", "Denticula",
  "Planothidium", "Frustulia", "Gomphonema", "Cocconeis", "Diploneis",
  "Amphora"
)

# 3. Apply the rules.
diatom_taxontable <- diatom_taxa %>%
  mutate(
    clean = variablename %>%
      str_remove_all("\\b(cf|aff)\\.\\s*") %>% # rule 1
      str_remove("\\s+(var|subsp)\\..*$") %>% # rule 2
      str_squish(),
    genus = word(clean, 1),
    epithet = word(clean, 2),
    # rule 3: keep genus + species only if the second word looks like a species
    harmonizedname = if_else(
      !is.na(epithet) & str_detect(epithet, "^[a-z]+(-[a-z]+)?$"),
      paste(genus, epithet), genus
    ),
    habit = case_when(
      genus %in% planktonic ~ "planktonic",
      genus %in% tychoplanktonic ~ "tychoplanktonic",
      genus %in% benthic ~ "benthic",
      TRUE ~ NA_character_
    )
  ) %>%
  select(taxonid, variablename, ecologicalgroup, harmonizedname, genus, habit)

readr::write_csv(diatom_taxontable, "data/diatom_taxontable.csv")
```

I did that work ahead of time, so we can read the table in. You can open it, change the rules or the `habit` column, and save it under a different file name to make it your own:

<th>habit<\/th>\n    <\/tr>\n  <\/thead>\n<\/table>","options":{"scrollX":"100%","dom":"tp","pageLength":8,"columnDefs":[{"className":"dt-right","targets":0},{"name":"taxonid","targets":0},{"name":"variablename","targets":1},{"name":"ecologicalgroup","targets":2},{"name":"harmonizedname","targets":3},{"name":"genus","targets":4},{"name":"habit","targets":5}],"order":[],"autoWidth":false,"orderClasses":false,"lengthMenu":[8,10,25,50,100]}},"evals":[],"jsHooks":[]}</script>

The 1107 original diatom names became 857 harmonized names, in 122 genera.

You can see we've changed some of the taxon names in the taxon table.  To replace the names in the `samples()` output, we'll join the two tables using an `inner_join()` (meaning the `variablename` must appear in both tables for the result to be included, which also drops the water chemistry rows), and then we're going to select only those elements of the sample tables that are relevant to our later analysis, using the `harmonizedname` column as our new name for the taxa:


``` r
all_samp <- samples(aggregate_dl)

all_samp <- all_samp %>%
  inner_join(translation, by = c("variablename" = "variablename")) %>%
  dplyr::select(!c("variablename")) %>%
  group_by(
    siteid, sitename, harmonizedname, genus, habit,
    sampleid, units, age,
    agetype, depth, datasetid,
    long, lat
  ) %>%
  summarise(value = sum(value), .groups = "keep")
```


We now have a cleaner set of taxon names compared to the original table, both because of harmonization (varieties and uncertain identifications are folded into their species), and because the join cleared out the non-diatom rows. Plotting the same set of taxa with the new harmonized names results in this plot:

![**Figure**. *The same site/sample plot as above, with with the new harmonized taxonomy. Note that the distribution of points along the curve is smoother, as we remove some of the taxonomic issues.*](simple_workflow_files/figure-html/origTableOut-1.png)

## 4. Simple Analytics

### 4.1. Stratigraphic Plotting

The aggregate sites above are mostly *surface samples*: one modern sample per lake, which is exactly what you want for a training set. To see change through time we need a **sediment core**, with many samples at different depths and ages. In section 3.3 we already found the diatom cores in the Philadelphia area with a known chronology (`penn_cores`). Four of them come from Barnegat Bay, on the New Jersey coast. We downloaded these ahead of time as well:


``` r
## This line is commented out because we've already run it for you.
# nolint start
## core_dl <- penn_cores %>% get_downloads(all_data = TRUE)
## saveRDS(core_dl, "data/diatom_core_download.RDS")
# nolint end
core_dl <- readRDS("data/diatom_core_download.RDS")
```

Before choosing a core, it helps to know that each chronology belongs to a collection unit. `chronologies()` gives us the chronology used by each dataset, so we can see how each core was dated:


``` r
chronologies(core_dl) %>%
  as.data.frame() %>%
  dplyr::select(siteid, datasetid, chronologyname, isdefault) %>%
  DT::datatable(rownames = FALSE, options = list(scrollX = "100%", dom = "t"))
```

```{=html}
<div class="datatables html-widget html-fill-item" id="htmlwidget-91696624d7010b6beee5" style="width:100%;height:auto;"></div>
<script type="application/json" data-for="htmlwidget-91696624d7010b6beee5">{"x":{"filter":"none","vertical":false,"data":[[14268,13321,13319,26232,14270,14269,15900],[22333,19880,19876,49030,22339,22336,24474],["Velinsky 2011","Collection date","Collection date","Collection date","Velinsky 2011","Velinsky 2011","Velinsky 2011"],[true,true,true,true,true,true,true]],"container":"<table class=\"display\">\n  <thead>\n    <tr>\n      <th>siteid<\/th>\n      <th>datasetid<\/th>\n      <th>chronologyname<\/th>\n      <th>isdefault<\/th>\n    <\/tr>\n  <\/thead>\n<\/table>","options":{"scrollX":"100%","dom":"t","columnDefs":[{"className":"dt-right","targets":[0,1]},{"name":"siteid","targets":0},{"name":"datasetid","targets":1},{"name":"chronologyname","targets":2},{"name":"isdefault","targets":3}],"order":[],"autoWidth":false,"orderClasses":false}},"evals":[],"jsHooks":[]}</script>
```

We can use packages like `rioja` to do stratigraphic plotting for a single record, but first we need to do some different data management.  Although we could do harmonization again we're going to simply take the most common taxa at a single site and plot them in a stratigraphic diagram. We pick the Barnegat Bay core at West Creek (`siteid` 14270).


``` r
# Get a particular site, in this case we are simply subsetting the
# `core_dl` object:
plotting_site <- neotoma2::filter(core_dl, siteid == 14270)

# Select only diatoms counted as valves (NISP) and convert to a "wide"
# table, using proportions. The first column will be "age".
# This turns our "long" table into a "wide" table:
counts <- plotting_site %>%
  samples() %>%
  toWide(
    ecologicalgroup = c("DIAT"),
    unit = c("NISP"),
    elementtypes = c("valve"),
    groupby = "age",
    operation = "prop"
  )

# Keep the age column, plus the taxa that make up at least 3% of three or more
# samples (the ages here include negative values, after 1950, so we do not
# test the age column itself):
common <- colSums(counts[, -1] > 0.03, na.rm = TRUE) >= 3
counts <- counts[, c(TRUE, common)]
```

Hopefully the code is pretty straightforward. The `toWide()` function provides you with significant control over the taxa, units and other elements of your data before you get them into the wide matrix (`age` by `taxon`) that most statistical tools such as the `vegan` package or `rioja` use.

To plot the data we can use `rioja`'s `strat.plot()`, sorting the taxa using weighted averaging scores (`wa.order`). I've also added a CONISS plot to the edge of the the plot, to show how the new *wide* data frame works with distance metric funcitons.


``` r
# Perform constrained clustering:
clust <- rioja::chclust(dist(sqrt(counts[, -1])),
  method = "coniss"
)

# Plot the stratigraphic plot, converting proportions to percentages:
plot <- rioja::strat.plot(counts[, -1] * 100,
  yvar = counts$age,
  title = "",
  ylabel = "Calibrated Years BP (negative = after 1950)",
  xlabel = "Diatoms (% of valves counted)",
  y.rev = TRUE,
  clust = clust,
  wa.order = "topleft",
  scale.percent = TRUE,
  yTop = 0.62, # leave room for the taxon names
  cex.xlabel = 0.8
)

rioja::addClustZone(plot, clust, 2, col = "red")
mtext(plotting_site[[1]]$sitename,
  side = 3, line = -1.5, adj = 0.02, font = 2, outer = TRUE
)
```

![](simple_workflow_files/figure-html/plotStrigraph-1.png)<!-- -->

### 4.2. Change in Time Across Sites

We now have several cores from the same bay with samples and with taxon names. I'm interested in looking at how the **kind of diatoms** changes across time: the share of the assemblage made up of benthic, tychoplanktonic and planktonic forms. This is where the `habit` column of our harmonization table is useful, since it lets us look at a change in the assemblage without having to read through hundreds of species. We keep only the cores that have at least five samples.


``` r
# Join the habit from the translation table, then sum the counts of each habit
# within every sample:
habit_by_sample <- samples(core_dl) %>%
  inner_join(translation, by = c("variablename" = "variablename")) %>%
  mutate(habit = dplyr::coalesce(habit, "not classified")) %>%
  group_by(siteid, sitename, datasetid, sampleid, age, habit) %>%
  summarise(value = sum(value), .groups = "drop_last") %>%
  mutate(proportion = value / sum(value)) %>%
  ungroup()

# Keep the cores that have at least five samples:
long_cores <- habit_by_sample %>%
  group_by(datasetid) %>%
  dplyr::filter(n_distinct(sampleid) >= 5) %>%
  ungroup()

# And then plot! One panel per habit, one line per core:
ggplot(
  dplyr::filter(long_cores, habit != "not classified"),
  aes(x = age, y = proportion, color = sitename)
) +
  geom_line() +
  geom_point() +
  facet_wrap(~habit, scales = "free_y") +
  scale_x_reverse() +
  scale_y_continuous(labels = scales::percent) +
  xlab("Calibrated Years BP (negative = after 1950)") +
  ylab("Share of valves counted") +
  labs(color = "Core") +
  guides(color = guide_legend(nrow = 2)) +
  theme_bw() +
  theme(legend.position = "bottom")
```

![](simple_workflow_files/figure-html/summarizeByTime-1.png)<!-- -->

Benthic diatoms make up most of these estuarine assemblages, and planktonic forms are rare. We can see whether the four cores change in a similar way, and which habit drives the changes. Note that the shares are out of all the valves counted, and the taxa that are not in our habit lookup are left out of the plot, so the three panels do not add up to 100%. Depending on how we divide the data we can also look at other dimensions, such as distance from the inlet, to better understand how the bay changed.

### 4.3. Diatoms and Water Chemistry (pH)

One of the most common uses of diatoms is to understand **water quality**: many diatoms prefer particular pH or nutrient conditions, and that is why training sets pair diatom surface samples with measured water chemistry. We downloaded both for each site in our aggregate slice. Now we can look at them together.

We first take the pH measured at each site. We average the values when a site has more than one, and drop values of 0, which in this data are missing measurements rather than real pH:


``` r
site_ph <- samples(aggregate_dl) %>%
  dplyr::filter(
    datasettype == "water chemistry",
    variablename == "pH", value > 0
  ) %>%
  group_by(siteid) %>%
  summarise(pH = mean(value), .groups = "drop")
```

We have a pH value for 55 sites. Water chemistry data in Neotoma may hold other variables too: total phosphorus, specific conductance and nitrogen, to name a few. Use `unique(...$variablename)` on the water chemistry rows to see what is available, and keep an eye on the `units` column, since the same variable is sometimes reported in different units (for example mg/L and µg/L).

Next, we take the diatom surface samples and add them up by genus, using the `genus` column of our translation table. We turn the counts into the proportion of each sample, and keep the genera that make up at least 1% of a sample in a good number of sites:


``` r
genus_by_site <- samples(aggregate_dl) %>%
  dplyr::filter(datasettype == "diatom surface sample") %>%
  inner_join(translation, by = c("variablename" = "variablename")) %>%
  group_by(siteid, sampleid, genus) %>%
  summarise(value = sum(value), .groups = "drop_last") %>%
  mutate(proportion = value / sum(value)) %>%
  group_by(siteid, genus) %>%
  summarise(proportion = mean(proportion), .groups = "drop") %>%
  inner_join(site_ph, by = "siteid")

# Genera that reach at least 1% at 15 or more sites with a pH value:
common_genera <- genus_by_site %>%
  dplyr::filter(proportion >= 0.01) %>%
  count(genus) %>%
  dplyr::filter(n >= 15) %>%
  pull(genus)
```

#### 4.3.1. Setting the Background

We want to get the background distribution of pH across all of the sites, to plot our taxon distributions against. This is the set of conditions that are available in the training set, and a genus that prefers certain conditions should look different from it.


``` r
background <- site_ph
```

Now we're going to plot it out, using `facet_wrap()` to plot each genus in its own panel. In each panel, the black line is the pH of all the sites, and the red line is the pH of the sites where that genus makes up at least 1% of the sample:


``` r
ggplot() +
  geom_density(
    data = dplyr::filter(genus_by_site, genus %in% common_genera, proportion >= 0.01),
    aes(x = pH), col = 2
  ) +
  facet_wrap(~genus) +
  geom_density(data = background, aes(x = pH)) +
  xlab("pH") +
  ylab("Kernel Density") +
  theme_bw()
```

![](simple_workflow_files/figure-html/ggplot-1.png)<!-- -->

## 5. Conclusion

So, we've done a lot in this example.  We've (1) searched for sites using site names and geographic parameters, (2) filtered results by dataset type and by whether a record has a chronology, (3) read a list of site IDs from a spreadsheet and used it to find the data we wanted, (4) obtained sample information for the selected datasets, cleaned up the diatom taxonomy, and (5) performed basic analysis including a stratigraphic diagram, a look at the change in planktonic diatoms through time, and a link between diatoms and pH. Hopefully you can use these examples as templates for your own future work, or as a building block for something new and cool!

## 6. Installing packages on your own {#localinstall}

We use several packages in this document, including `leaflet`, `sf` and others. We load the packages using the `pacman` package, which will automatically install the packages if they do not currently exist in your set of packages.


``` r
options(warn = -1)
pacman::p_load(
  neotoma2, dplyr, ggplot2, sf, geojsonsf, leaflet, DT, readr, readxl,
  stringr, rioja
)
```

This workflow uses the development version of `neotoma2` (version 1.1.1.9000 or later), which handles long lists of site IDs and can search for several dataset types at once. You can install it with:

```r
install.packages("remotes")
remotes::install_github("NeotomaDB/neotoma2", ref = "dev")
```

Note that R is sensitive to the order in which packages are loaded.  Using `neotoma2::` tells R explicitly that you want to use the `neotoma2` package to run a particular function. So, for a function like `filter()`, which exists in other packages such as `dplyr`, you may see an error that looks like:

```bash
Error in UseMethod("filter") : 
  no applicable method for 'filter' applied to an object of class "sites"
```
